• Synthetics. On important synthetic tasks such as copying and induction heads that have been proposed as being key to large language models, Mamba not only solves them easily but can extrapolate solutions indefinitely long (>1M tokens).

Mamba out-performs prior state-of-the-art models such as SaShiMi, Hyena, and Transformers on modelin<sub>g</sub> audio waveforms and DNA se<sub>q</sub>uences, both in <sub>p</sub>retrainin<sub>g q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> and downstream metrics (e.<sub>g</sub>. reducin<sub>g</sub> FID on a challenging speech generation dataset by more than half). • Language Modeling. Discretization. The first sta e transforms the “continuous arameters” ${ \bf \Sigma } ^ { , } ( \Delta , A , B )$ t<sub>o</sub> “di<sub>scre</sub>t<sub>e arame</sub>t<sub>ers</sub>” $\mathbf { \overline { { \nabla } } } ( \overline { { A } } , \overline { { B } } )$ th<sub>roug</sub>h fi<sub>xe</sub>d f<sub>ormu</sub>l<sub>as</sub> $\overline { { { \cal A } } } = f _ { A } ( \Delta , A )$ <sub>an</sub>d $\overline { { B } } = f _ { B } ( \Delta , A , B )$ <sub>, w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> th<sub>e pa</sub>i<sub>r</sub> $( f _ { A } , f _ { B } )$ is called a discretization rule. \overline {{{A}}} = \exp (\Delta A) \quad \overline {{{B}}} = (\Delta A) ^ {- 1} (\exp (\Delta A) - I) \cdot \Delta B\tag{4}
$$

Discretization has dee<sub>p</sub> connections to continuous-time s<sub>y</sub>stems which can endow them with additional <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>erties such as resolution invariance (N u en Goel et al. 2022) and automaticall ensurin that the model is ro erl normalized (Gu, Johnson, Timalsina, et al. 2023; Orvieto et al. 2023). It also has connections to <sub>g</sub>atin<sub>g</sub> mechanisms of RNNs (Gu, Gulcehre, et al. 2020; Tallec and Ollivier 2018) which we will revisit in Section 3.5. However, from a mechanical <sub>p</sub>oint of view discretization can sim<sub>p</sub>l<sub>y</sub> be viewed as the first ste<sub>p</sub> of the com<sub>pu</sub>tation <sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>h in the forward <sub>p</sub>ass of an SSM. Alternate flavors of SSMs can b<sub>yp</sub>ass the discretization ste<sub>p</sub> and <sub>p</sub>arameterize $( { \overline { { A } } } , { \bar { B } } )$ directl<sub>y</sub> instead (Zhan<sub>g</sub> et al. 2023), <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>may</sub> b<sub>e eas</sub>i<sub>er</sub> t<sub>o reason a</sub>b<sub>ou</sub>t<sub>.</sub>

Computation. After the parameters have been transformed from $( \Delta , A , B , C ) \mapsto ( { \overline { { A } } } , { \overline { { B } } } , C )$ <sub>,</sub> th<sub>e mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>can</sub> b<sub>e compu</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d in two ways, either as a linear recurrence (2) or a global convolution (3).

Commonl<sub>y</sub>, the model uses the convolutional mode (3) for eficient <sub>p</sub>arallelizable trainin<sub>g</sub> (where the whole in<sub>p</sub>ut se<sub>q</sub>uence is seen ahead of time), and switched into recurrent mode (2) for eficient autore<sub>g</sub>ressive inference (where the in<sub>p</sub>uts are seen one timeste<sub>p</sub> at a time).

Linear Time Invariance (LTI). An important property of equations (1) to (3) is that the model’s dynamics are constant through time. In other words (Δ, �, �, �), and consequently (�, �) as well, are fixed for all time-steps. This property is called linear time invariance (LTI), which is deeply connected to recurrence and convolutions. Informally, we think of LTI SSMs as bein<sub>g</sub> e<sub>q</sub>uivalent to an<sub>y</sub> linear recurrence (2a) or convolution (3b), and use LTI as an umbrella term for these <sub>c</sub>l<sub>asses o</sub>f <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s.</sub>

Thus far, all structured SSMs have been LTI (e.<sub>g</sub>. com<sub>p</sub>uted as convolutions) because of fundamental eficienc<sub>y</sub> constraints, discussed in Section 3.3. However<sub>,</sub> a core insi<sub>g</sub>ht of this work is that LTI models have fundamental limitations in modelin<sub>g</sub> certain t<sub>yp</sub>es of data<sub>,</sub> and our technical contributions involve removin<sub>g</sub> the LTI constraint while overcomin<sub>g</sub> the eficienc<sub>y</sub> b<sub>o</sub>ttl<sub>enec</sub>k<sub>s.</sub>

Structure and Dimensions. Finally, we note that structured SSMs are so named because computing them eficiently also requires imposing structure on the � matrix. The most popular form of structure is diagonal (Gu, Gupta, et al. 2022; Gu<sub>p</sub>ta, Gu, and Berant 2022; Smith, Warrin<sub>g</sub>ton, and Linderman 2023), which we also use.

General State Space Models. We note that the term state space model has a very broad meaning which simply represents th<sub>e</sub> <sub>no</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>any</sub> <sub>recurren</sub>t <sub>process</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>a</sub> l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e.</sub> It h<sub>as</sub> b<sub>een</sub> <sub>use</sub>d t<sub>o</sub> <sub>re</sub>f<sub>er</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>many</sub> di<sub>spara</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>concep</sub>t<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub> dif<sub>eren</sub>t disci<sub>p</sub>lines, includin<sub>g</sub> Markov decision <sub>p</sub>rocesses (MDP) (reinforcement learnin<sub>g</sub> (Hafner et al. 2020)), d<sub>y</sub>namic causal modelin<sub>g</sub> (DCM) (com<sub>p</sub>utational neuroscience (Friston, Harrison, and Penn<sub>y</sub> 2003)), Kalman filters (controls (Kalman 1960)), hidden Markov models (HMM) and linear d<sub>y</sub>namical s<sub>y</sub>stems (LDS) (machine learnin<sub>g</sub>), and recurrent (and sometimes convolutional) models at lar<sub>g</sub>e (dee<sub>p</sub> learnin<sub>g</sub>).

For convenience we ma<sub>y</sub> also include derivatives of such models, such as those focusin<sub>g</sub> on either the linear-recurrence or <sub>g</sub>lobal-convolution view<sub>p</sub>oints (Y. Li et al. 2023; Orvieto et al. 2023; Poli et al. 2023), and clarif<sub>y</sub> nuances when necessar<sub>y</sub>.

SSM Architectures. SSMs are standalone sequence transformations that can be incorporated into end-to-end neural network architectures. (We also sometimes call SSM architectures SSNNs which are to SSM la ers as CNNs are to linear convolution la<sub>y</sub>ers.) We discuss some of the most well-known SSM architectures, man<sub>y</sub> of which will also serve as our <sub>p</sub>rimar<sub>y</sub> baselines.

• Linear attention (Katharo<sub>p</sub>oulos et al. 2020) is an a<sub>pp</sub>roximation of self-attention involvin<sub>g</sub> a recurrence which can be <sub>v</sub>i<sub>ewe</sub>d <sub>as a</sub> d<sub>ege</sub>n<sub>e</sub>r<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> lin<sub>ea</sub>r SSM<sub>.</sub>

• H3 (Dao, Fu, Saab, et al. 2023) eneralized this recurrence to use S4; it can be viewed as an architecture with an SSM sandwiched b<sub>y</sub> two <sub>g</sub>ated connections (Fi<sub>g</sub>ure 3). H3 also inserts a standard local convolution, which the<sub>y</sub> frame as a <sub>s</sub>hift-SSM<sub>,</sub> b<sub>e</sub>f<sub>o</sub>r<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> m<sub>a</sub>in SSM l<sub>aye</sub>r<sub>.</sub>

• H<sub>y</sub>ena (Poli et al. 2023) uses the same architecture as H3 but re<sub>p</sub>laces the S4 la<sub>y</sub>er with an MLP-<sub>p</sub>arameterized <sub>g</sub>lobal convolution (Romero et al. 2021).

• RetNet (Y. Sun et al. 2023) adds an additional ate to the architecture and uses a sim ler SSM, allowin an alternative <sub>p</sub>arallelizable com<sub>p</sub>utation <sub>p</sub>ath, usin<sub>g</sub> a variant of multi-head attention (MHA) instead of convolutions.

• RWKV (B. Pen<sub>g</sub> et al. 2023) is a recent RNN desi<sub>g</sub>ned for lan<sub>g</sub>ua<sub>g</sub>e modelin<sub>g</sub> based on another linear attention a<sub>pp</sub>roximation, the attention-free Transformer (S. Zhai et al. 2021). Its main “WKV” mechanism involves LTI recurrences and <sub>ca</sub>n b<sub>e v</sub>i<sub>ewe</sub>d <sub>as</sub> th<sub>e</sub> r<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>o o</sub>f t<sub>wo</sub> SSM<sub>s</sub>

Other closel<sub>y</sub> related SSMs and architectures are discussed further in an extended related work (A<sub>pp</sub>endix B). We hi<sub>g</sub>hli<sub>g</sub>ht in <sub>p</sub>articular S5 (Smith, Warrin<sub>g</sub>ton, and Linderman 2023), QRNN (Bradbur<sub>y</sub> et al. 2016), and SRU (Lei et al. 2017), which <sub>we v</sub>i<sub>ew as</sub> th<sub>e mos</sub>t <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ose</sub>l<sub>y re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>me</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d<sub>s</sub> t<sub>o our core se</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> SSM<sub>.</sub>

## 3 Selective State Space Models

We motivate our selection mechanism usin<sub>g</sub> intuition from s<sub>y</sub>nthetic tasks (Section 3.1), then ex<sub>p</sub>lain how to incor<sub>p</sub>orate this mechanism into state s<sub>p</sub>ace models (Section 3.2). The resultin<sub>g</sub> time-var<sub>y</sub>in<sub>g</sub> SSMs cannot use convolutions, <sub>p</sub>resentin<sub>g</sub> <sub>a</sub> t<sub>ec</sub>h<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>c</sub>h<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>enge o</sub>f h<sub>ow</sub> t<sub>o compu</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>em e</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>en</sub>tl<sub>y.</sub> W<sub>e overcome</sub> thi<sub>s w</sub>ith <sub>a</sub> h<sub>ar</sub>d<sub>ware-aware a</sub>l<sub>gor</sub>ith<sub>m</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>exp</sub>l<sub>o</sub>it<sub>s</sub> the memor hierarch on modern hardware (Section 3.3). We then describe a sim le SSM architecture without attention or even MLP blocks (Section 3.4). Finall<sub>y</sub>, we discuss some additional <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>erties of selection mechanisms (Section 3.5).

## 3.1 Motivation: Selection as a Means of Compression

We argue that a fundamental problem of sequence modeling is compressing context into a smaller state. In fact, we can <sub>v</sub>i<sub>ew</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>ra</sub>d<sub>eo</sub>f<sub>s</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>popu</sub>l<sub>ar</sub> <sub>sequence</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> <sub>po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f <sub>v</sub>i<sub>ew.</sub> F<sub>or</sub> <sub>examp</sub>l<sub>e,</sub> <sub>a</sub>tt<sub>en</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> i<sub>s</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th <sub>e</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> <sub>an</sub>d i<sub>ne</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>en</sub>t b<sub>ecause</sub> it <sub>exp</sub>li<sub>c</sub>itl<sub>y</sub> d<sub>oes no</sub>t <sub>compress con</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t <sub>a</sub>t <sub>a</sub>ll<sub>.</sub> Thi<sub>s can</sub> b<sub>e seen</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>ac</sub>t th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>au</sub>t<sub>oregress</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>erence</sub> re<sub>q</sub>uires ex<sub>p</sub>licitl<sub>y</sub> storin<sub>g</sub> the entire context (i.e. the KV cache), which directl<sub>y</sub> causes the slow linear-time inference and <sub>qua</sub>d<sub>ra</sub>ti<sub>c-</sub>ti<sub>me</sub> t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng o</sub>f T<sub>rans</sub>f<sub>ormers.</sub> O<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e o</sub>th<sub>er</sub> h<sub>an</sub>d<sub>, recurren</sub>t <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s are e</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>en</sub>t b<sub>ecause</sub> th<sub>ey</sub> h<sub>ave a</sub> fi<sub>n</sub>it<sub>e s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e,</sub> im<sub>p</sub>l<sub>y</sub>in<sub>g</sub> constant-time inference and linear-time trainin<sub>g</sub>. However<sub>,</sub> their efectiveness is limited b<sub>y</sub> how well this state h<sub>as compresse</sub>d th<sub>e con</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t<sub>.</sub>

To understand this <sub>p</sub>rinci<sub>p</sub>le, we focus on two runnin<sub>g</sub> exam<sub>p</sub>les of s<sub>y</sub>nthetic tasks (Fi<sub>g</sub>ure 2).

• The Selective Copying task modifies the popular Copying task (Arjovsky, Shah, and Bengio 2016) by varying the position of the tokens to memorize. It requires content-aware reasoning to be able to memorize the relevant tokens (colored) and filter out the irrelevant ones (white).

• The Induction Heads task is a well-known mechanism hypothesized to explain the majority of in-context learning abilities of LLMs (Olsson et al. 2022). It requires context-aware reasoning to know when to produce the correct output in the appropriate context (black).

These tasks reveal the failure mode of LTI models. From the recurrent view, their constant d<sub>y</sub>namics (e.<sub>g</sub>. the $( { \overline { { A } } } , { \overline { { B } } } )$ transitions in (2)) cannot let them select the correct information from their context, or afect the hidden state <sub>p</sub>assed alon<sub>g</sub> the se<sub>q</sub>uence in an in<sub>p</sub>ut-de<sub>p</sub>endent wa<sub>y</sub>. From the convolutional view<sub>,</sub> it is known that <sub>g</sub>lobal convolutions can solve the vanilla Co<sub>py</sub>in<sub>g</sub> task (Romero et al. 2021) because it onl<sub>y</sub> re<sub>q</sub>uires time-awareness, but that the<sub>y</sub> have dificult<sub>y</sub> with the Selective Co<sub>py</sub>in<sub>g</sub> task because of lack of content-awareness (Fi<sub>g</sub>ure 2). More concretel<sub>y</sub>, the s<sub>p</sub>acin<sub>g</sub> between in<sub>p</sub>uts-to-out<sub>p</sub>uts is var<sub>y</sub>in<sub>g</sub> and cannot be modeled b<sub>y</sub> static convolution kernels.

## 3.3 Eficient Implementation of Selective SSMs

Hardware-friendl<sub>y p</sub>rimitives such as convolutions (Krizhevsk<sub>y</sub>, Sutskever, and Hinton 2012) and attention (Bahdanau, Cho, and Bengio 2015; Vaswani et al. 2017) enjoy widespread application. Here we aim to make selective SSMs eficient on modern hardware (GPUs) as well. The selection mechanism is <sub>q</sub>uite natural, and earlier works attem<sub>p</sub>ted to incor<sub>p</sub>orate s<sub>p</sub>ecial cases of selection, such as lettin<sub>g</sub> Δ var<sub>y</sub> over time in recurrent SSMs (Gu, Dao, et al. 2020). However, as <sub>p</sub>reviousl<sub>y</sub> mentioned a core limitation in the <sub>u</sub>sa<sub>g</sub>e of SSMs is their com<sub>pu</sub>tational eficienc<sub>y, w</sub>hich <sub>w</sub>as <sub>w</sub>h<sub>y</sub> S4 and all deri<sub>v</sub>ati<sub>v</sub>es used LTI (non-selective) models, most commonl<sub>y</sub> in the form of <sub>g</sub>lobal convolutions.

## 3.3.1 Motivation of Prior Models

We first revisit this motivation and overview our a<sub>pp</sub>roach to overcome limitations of <sub>p</sub>rior methods.

<sub>•</sub> At <sub>a</sub> hi<sub>g</sub>h l<sub>eve</sub>l<sub>, recurren</sub>t <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s suc</sub>h <sub>as</sub> SSM<sub>s a</sub>l<sub>ways</sub> b<sub>a</sub>l<sub>ance a</sub> t<sub>ra</sub>d<sub>eo</sub>f b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ween express</sub>i<sub>v</sub>it<sub>y an</sub>d <sub>spee</sub>d<sub>: as</sub> di<sub>scusse</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> Section 3.1, models with larger hidden state dimension should be more efective but slower. Thus we want to maximize hidden state dimension without paying speed and memory costs.

• Note that the recurrent mode is more flexible than the convolution mode, since the latter (3) is derived from ex<sub>p</sub>andin<sub>g</sub> the former (2) (Gu, Goel, and Ré 2022; Gu, Johnson, Goel, et al. 2021). However, this would re<sub>q</sub>uire com<sub>p</sub>utin<sub>g</sub> and materializin<sub>g</sub> the latent state ℎ with sha<sub>p</sub>e (B, L, D, N), which is much lar<sub>g</sub>er (b<sub>y</sub> a factor of �, the SSM state dimension) than the in<sub>p</sub>ut � and out<sub>p</sub>ut <sub>�</sub> of sha<sub>p</sub>e (B, L, D). Thus the more eficient convolution mode was introduced which could b<sub>yp</sub>ass the state com<sub>p</sub>utation and materializes a convolution kernel (3a) of size onl<sub>y</sub> (B, L, D).

Section 4.3.2).

Boundary Resetting. In settings where multiple independent sequences are stitched together, Transformers can keep th<sub>em se ara</sub>t<sub>e</sub> b i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>an</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>n a ar</sub>ti<sub>cu</sub>l<sub>ar a</sub>tt<sub>en</sub>ti<sub>on mas</sub>k<sub>, w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> LTI <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s w</sub>ill bl<sub>ee</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>orma</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ween</sub> th<sub>e se uences.</sub> Selective SSMs can also reset their state at boundaries (e.<sub>g</sub>. $\Delta _ { t } \to \infty ,$ <sub>, or</sub> Th<sub>eorem</sub> 1 <sub>w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> $g _ { t } \to 1 )$ . Additi<sub>ona</sub>ll<sub>y,</sub> <sub>we</sub> <sub>e</sub>l<sub>a</sub>b<sub>ora</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>on</sub> <sub>e</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>eac</sub>h <sub>se</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> <sub>parame</sub>t<sub>er.</sub>

Interpretation of $\Delta .$ In <sub>g</sub>eneral, Δ controls the balance between how much to focus or i<sub>g</sub>nore the current in<sub>p</sub>ut $x _ { t } .$ . It <sub>g</sub>eneralizes RNN <sub>g</sub>ates (e.<sub>g</sub>. <sub>��</sub> in Theorem 1): mechanicall<sub>y</sub>, a lar<sub>g</sub>e Δ resets the state ℎ and focuses on the current in<sub>p</sub>ut $x ,$ while a small Δ <sub>p</sub>ersists the state and i<sub>g</sub>nores the current in<sub>p</sub>ut. SSMs (1)-(2) can be inter<sub>p</sub>reted as a continuous s<sub>y</sub>stem discretized b<sub>y</sub> a timeste<sub>p</sub> $\Delta ,$ and in this context the int<sub>u</sub>ition is that lar<sub>g</sub>e $\Delta \to \infty$ re<sub>p</sub>resents the s<sub>y</sub>stem focusin<sub>g</sub> on the current in<sub>p</sub>ut for lon<sub>g</sub>er (thus “selectin<sub>g</sub>” it and for<sub>g</sub>ettin<sub>g</sub> its current state) while a small $\Delta  0$ re<sub>p</sub>resents a trans<sup>i</sup>ent in<sub>p</sub>ut that is i<sub>g</sub>nored.

Interpretation of �. We remark that while the � parameter could also be selective, it ultimately afects the model onl<sub>y</sub> throu<sub>g</sub>h its interaction with Δ via $\overline { { A } } = \exp ( \Delta A )$ (the discretization (4)). Thus selectivit<sub>y</sub> in Δ is enou<sub>g</sub>h to ensure <sub>se</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>v</sub>it<sub>y</sub> in $( \overline { { A } } , \overline { { B } } )$ , and is the main source of im rovement. We h othesize that makin � selective in addition to (or instead of) Δ would have similar <sub>p</sub>erformance, and leave it out for sim<sub>p</sub>licit<sub>y</sub>.

Interpretation of � and �. As discussed in Section 3.1, the most important property of selectivity is filtering out irrelevant information so that a se<sub>qu</sub>ence model’s context can be com<sub>p</sub>ressed into an eficient state. In an SSM<sub>,</sub> modif<sub>y</sub>in<sub>g</sub> � and � to be selective allows finer-grained control over whether to let an input $x _ { t }$ i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> $h _ { t } ,$ <sub>, or</sub> th<sub>e s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sup>out</sup>p<sup>ut</sup> $y _ { t }$ . These can be inter<sub>p</sub>reted as allowin<sub>g</sub> the model to modulate the recurrent d<sub>y</sub>namics based on content (in<sub>p</sub>ut) and context (hidden states) res<sub>p</sub>ectivel<sub>y</sub>.

## 3.6 Additional Model Details

Real vs. Complex. Most prior SSMs use complex numbers in their state $h ,$ which is necessar<sub>y</sub> for stron<sub>g p</sub>erformance on man<sub>y</sub> tasks in <sub>p</sub>erce<sub>p</sub>tual modalities (Gu, Goel, and Ré 2022). However, it has been em<sub>p</sub>iricall<sub>y</sub> observed that com<sub>p</sub>letel<sub>y</sub> real-valued SSMs seem to work fine, and <sub>p</sub>ossibl<sub>y</sub> even better, in some settin<sub>g</sub>s (Ma et al. 2023). We use real values as th<sub>e</sub> d<sub>e</sub>f<sub>au</sub>lt<sub>, w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>wor</sub>k <sub>we</sub>ll f<sub>or a</sub>ll b<sub>u</sub>t <sub>one o</sub>f <sub>our</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>s; we</sub> h<sub>ypo</sub>th<sub>es</sub>i<sub>ze</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t th<sub>e comp</sub>l<sub>ex-rea</sub>l t<sub>ra</sub>d<sub>eo</sub>f i<sub>s re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e</sub> continuous-discrete s<sub>p</sub>ectrum in data modalities, where com<sub>p</sub>lex numbers are hel<sub>p</sub>ful for continuous modalities (e.<sub>g</sub>. audio video) but not discrete (e.<sub>g</sub>. text, DNA).

Initialization. Most prior SSMs also suggest special initializations, particularly in the complex-valued case, which can hel<sub>p</sub> in several settin<sub>g</sub>s such as low-data re<sub>g</sub>imes. Our default initialization for the com<sub>p</sub>lex case is S4D-Lin and for the real case is S4D-Real (Gu Gu ta et al. 2022) which is based on the HIPPO theor (Gu Dao et al. 2020). These define the �-th <sub>e</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f $A { \mathrm { a s } } - 1 / 2 +$ <sub>�</sub>� <sub>an</sub>d $- ( n + 1 )$ res<sub>p</sub>ectivel<sub>y</sub>. However<sub>,</sub> we ex<sub>p</sub>ect man<sub>y</sub> initializations to work fine<sub>,</sub> <sub>p</sub>articularl<sub>y</sub> in th<sub>e</sub> l<sub>arge-</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a an</sub>d <sub>rea</sub>l<sub>-va</sub>l<sub>ue</sub>d SSM <sub>reg</sub>i<sub>mes; some a</sub>bl<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ons are cons</sub>id<sub>ere</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> S<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> 4<sub>.</sub>6<sub>.</sub>

Parameterization of Δ. We defined the selective adjustment to $\Delta$ as $s _ { \Delta } ( x ) = { \tt B r o a d c a s t } _ { D } ( { \tt L i n e a r } _ { 1 } ( x ) )$ <sub>, w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>was</sub> motivated b<sub>y</sub> the mechanics of Δ (Section 3.5). We observe that it can be <sub>g</sub>eneralized from dimension 1 to a lar<sub>g</sub>er dimension R. We set this to be a small fraction of D, which uses a ne<sub>g</sub>li<sub>g</sub>ible number of <sub>p</sub>arameters com<sub>p</sub>ared to the main Linear projections in t<sup>h</sup>e <sup>bl</sup>oc<sup>k</sup>. We a<sup>dd</sup>itiona<sup>ll</sup>y note t<sup>h</sup>at t<sup>h</sup>e <sup>b</sup>roa<sup>d</sup>casting operation can instea<sup>d</sup> <sup>b</sup>e viewe<sup>d</sup> as anot<sup>h</sup>er Linear projection, initia<sup>l</sup>ize<sup>d</sup> to a speci<sup>fi</sup>c pattern o<sup>f</sup> 1<sup>’</sup>s an<sup>d</sup> $0 ^ { \prime } s ;$ i<sup>f</sup> t<sup>h</sup>is projection is traina<sup>bl</sup>e, t<sup>h</sup>is <sup>l</sup>ea<sup>d</sup>s to t<sup>h</sup>e a<sup>l</sup>ternative $s _ { \Delta } ( x ) = \mathsf { L i n e a r } _ { D } ( \mathsf { L i n e a r } _ { R } ( x ) )$ , w<sup>h</sup>ic<sup>h</sup> can <sup>b</sup>e viewe<sup>d</sup> as a <sup>l</sup>ow-ran<sup>k</sup> projection.

In our ex<sub>p</sub>eriments, the Δ <sub>p</sub>arameter (which can be viewed as a bias term) is initialized to $\tau _ { \Delta } ^ { - 1 } ( \mathsf { U n i f o r m } ( [ 0 . 0 0 1 , 0 . 1 ] ) )$ followin<sub>g p</sub>rior work on SSMs (Gu, Johnson, Timalsina, et al. 2023).

Remark 3.1. For brevity in our experimental results, we sometimes abbreviate selective SSMs as S6 models, because they are S4 models with a selection mechanism and computed with a scan.

## 4.1 Synthetic Tasks

Full ex<sub>p</sub>eriment details for these tasks includin<sub>g</sub> task details and trainin<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>rotocol are in A<sub>pp</sub>endix E.1.

## 4.1.1 Selective Copying

The Co<sub>py</sub>in<sub>g</sub> task is one of the most well-studied s<sub>y</sub>nthetic tasks for se<sub>q</sub>uence modelin<sub>g,</sub> ori<sub>g</sub>inall<sub>y</sub> desi<sub>g</sub>ned to test the memorization abilities of recurrent models. As discussed in Section 3.1, LTI SSMs (linear recurrences and <sub>g</sub>lobal convolutions) can easil<sub>y</sub> solve this task b<sub>y</sub> onl<sub>y</sub> kee<sub>p</sub>in<sub>g</sub> track of time instead of reasonin<sub>g</sub> about the data; for exam<sub>p</sub>le, b<sub>y</sub> constructin<sub>g</sub> a convolution kernel of exactl<sub>y</sub> the ri<sub>g</sub>ht len<sub>g</sub>th (Fi<sub>g</sub>ure 2). This was ex<sub>p</sub>licitl<sub>y</sub> validated in earlier work on <sub>g</sub>lobal convolutions (Romero et al. 2021). The Selective Co<sub>py</sub>in<sub>g</sub> task <sub>p</sub>revents this shortcut b<sub>y</sub> randomizin<sub>g</sub> the s<sub>p</sub>acin<sub>g</sub> between tokens. Note that this task has been introduced before as the Denoisin<sub>g</sub> task (Jin<sub>g</sub> et al. 2019).

Note that man<sub>y p</sub>revious works ar<sub>g</sub>ue that addin<sub>g</sub> architecture <sub>g</sub>atin<sub>g</sub> (multi<sub>p</sub>licative interactions) can endow models with “data-de<sub>p</sub>endence” and solve related tasks (Dao, Fu, Saab, et al. 2023; Poli et al. 2023). However, we find this ex<sub>p</sub>lanation insuficient intuitivel<sub>y</sub> because such <sub>g</sub>atin<sub>g</sub> does not interact alon<sub>g</sub> the se<sub>q</sub>uence axis<sub>,</sub> and cannot afect the s<sub>p</sub>acin<sub>g</sub> between tokens. In <sub>p</sub>articular architecture <sub>g</sub>atin<sub>g</sub> is not an instance of a selection mechanism (A<sub>pp</sub>endix A).

T<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> 1 <sub>con</sub>fi<sub>rms</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>ga</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>arc</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ures suc</sub>h <sub>as</sub> H3 <sub>an</sub>d M<sub>am</sub>b<sub>a on</sub>l<sub>y par</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> i<sub>mprove per</sub>f<sub>ormance, w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e se</sub>l<sub>ec-</sub> tion mechanism (modif<sub>y</sub>in<sub>g</sub> S4 to S6) easil<sub>y</sub> solves this task, <sub>p</sub>articularl<sub>y</sub> when combined with these more <sub>p</sub>owerful <sub>arc</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ures.</sub>

## 4.1.2 Induction Heads

Induction heads (Olsson et al. 2022) is a sim<sub>p</sub>le task from the mechanistic inter<sub>p</sub>retabilit<sub>y</sub> lens (Elha<sub>g</sub>e et al. 2021) that is sur<sub>p</sub>risin<sub>g</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>redictive of the in-context learnin<sub>g</sub> abilit<sub>y</sub> of LLMs. It re<sub>q</sub>uires models to <sub>p</sub>erform associative recall and co<sub>py</sub>: f<sub>or</sub> <sub>examp</sub>l<sub>e,</sub> if th<sub>e</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l h<sub>as</sub> <sub>seen</sub> <sub>a</sub> bi<sub>gram</sub> <sub>suc</sub>h <sub>as</sub> “H<sub>arry</sub> P<sub>o</sub>tt<sub>er</sub>” i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>sequence,</sub> th<sub>en</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>nex</sub>t ti<sub>me</sub> “H<sub>arry</sub>” <sub>appears</sub> i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>same</sub> <sub>sequence,</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>s</sub>h<sub>ou</sub>ld b<sub>e</sub> <sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>t “P<sub>o</sub>tt<sub>er</sub>” b<sub>y</sub> <sub>copy</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> hi<sub>s</sub>t<sub>ory.</sub>

Dataset. We train a 2-layer model on the induction heads task at sequence length 256, with a vocab size of 16, which is com<sub>p</sub>arable to <sub>p</sub>rior work on this task (Dao, Fu, Saab, et al. 2023) but with lon<sub>g</sub>er se<sub>q</sub>uences. We additionall<sub>y</sub> investi<sub>g</sub>ate <sub>g</sub>eneralization and extra<sub>p</sub>olation abilities b<sub>y</sub> evaluatin<sub>g</sub> on a ran<sub>g</sub>e of se<sub>q</sub>uence len<sub>g</sub>ths from $2 ^ { 6 } = 6 4$ <sup>u</sup>p <sup>to</sup> $2 ^ { 2 0 } = 1 0 4 8 5 7 6$ at test time.

Models. Following established work on induction heads, we use 2 layer models, which allows attention to mechanistically solve the induction heads task (Olsson et al. 2022). We test both multi-head attention (8 heads, with various <sub>p</sub>ositional encodin<sub>g</sub>s) and SSM variants. We use a model dimension � of 64 for Mamba and 128 for the other models.

Results. Table 2 shows that Mamba—or more precisely, its selective SSM layer—has the ability to solve the task perfectly because of its ability to selectively remember the relevant token while ignoring everything else in between. It generalizes perfectly to million-length sequences, or 4000× longer than it saw during training, while no other method goes be<sub>y</sub>ond 2×.

W<sub>e</sub> <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> M<sub>am</sub>b<sub>a</sub> <sub>arc</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ure</sub> <sub>on</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>d<sub>ar</sub>d <sub>au</sub>t<sub>oregress</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> l<sub>anguage</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>li<sub>ng</sub> <sub>aga</sub>i<sub>ns</sub>t <sub>o</sub>th<sub>er</sub> <sub>arc</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ures,</sub> <sub>on</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th <sub>p</sub>retrainin<sub>g</sub> metrics (<sub>p</sub>er<sub>p</sub>lexit<sub>y</sub>) and zero-shot evaluations. Gao, Biderman, et al. 2020), and follow the trainin<sub>g</sub> reci<sub>p</sub>e described in Brown et al. (2020). All trainin<sub>g</sub> details are in A<sub>pp</sub>endix E.2.

## 4.2.1 Scaling Laws

For baselines, we com<sub>p</sub>are a<sub>g</sub>ainst the standard Transformer architecture (GPT3 architecture), as well as the stron<sub>g</sub>est Transformer reci<sub>p</sub>e we know of (here referred to as Transformer++), based on the PaLM and LLaMa architectures (e.<sub>g</sub>. rotar<sub>y</sub> embeddin<sub>g</sub>, SwiGLU MLP, RMSNorm instead of La<sub>y</sub>erNorm, no linear bias, and hi<sub>g</sub>her learnin<sub>g</sub> rates). We also com<sub>p</sub>are a<sub>g</sub>ainst other recent sub<sub>q</sub>uadratic architectures (Fi<sub>g</sub>ure 4). All model details are in A<sub>pp</sub>endix E.2.

Fi<sub>g</sub>ure 4 shows scalin<sub>g</sub> laws under the standard Chinchilla (Hofmann et al. 2022) <sub>p</sub>rotocol, on models from ≈ 125� to ≈ 1.3� parameters. Mamba is the first attention-free model to match the performance of a very strong Transformer recipe (Transformer++) that has now become standard, particularly as the sequence length grows. (We note th<sub>a</sub>t f<sub>u</sub>ll <sub>resu</sub>lt<sub>s on con</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t l<sub>eng</sub>th 8k <sub>are m</sub>i<sub>ss</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> f<sub>or</sub> th<sub>e</sub> RWKV <sub>an</sub>d R<sub>e</sub>tN<sub>e</sub>t b<sub>ase</sub>li<sub>nes, pr</sub>i<sub>or s</sub>t<sub>rong recurren</sub>t <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>can a</sub>l<sub>so</sub> b<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erpre</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>as</sub> SSM<sub>s,</sub> b<sub>ecause o</sub>f <sub>a</sub> l<sub>ac</sub>k <sub>o</sub>f <sub>e</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>en</sub>t i<sub>mp</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> l<sub>ea</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> t<sub>o ou</sub>t<sub>-o</sub>f<sub>-memory or unrea</sub>li<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>c</sub> com<sub>p</sub>utation re<sub>q</sub>uirements.)

## 4.3 DNA Modeling

M<sub>o</sub>ti<sub>va</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e success o</sub>f l<sub>arge</sub> l<sub>anguage mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s,</sub> th<sub>ere</sub> h<sub>as</sub> b<sub>een recen</sub>t <sub>exp</sub>l<sub>ora</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o us</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>p</sub>aradi<sub>g</sub>m for <sub>g</sub>enomics. DNA has been likened to lan<sub>g</sub>ua<sub>g</sub>e in that it consists of se<sub>q</sub>uences of discrete tokens with a finite vocabular<sub>y</sub>. It is also known for re<sub>q</sub>uirin<sub>g</sub> lon<sub>g</sub>-ran<sub>g</sub>e de<sub>p</sub>endencies to model (Avsec et al. 2021). We investi<sub>g</sub>ate Mamba as a FM backbone for <sub>p</sub>retrainin<sub>g</sub> and fine-tunin<sub>g</sub> in the same settin<sub>g</sub> as recent works on lon<sub>g</sub>-se<sub>q</sub>uence models for DNA (N<sub>g</sub>u<sub>y</sub>en, Poli, et al. 2023). In <sub>p</sub>articular, we focus on two ex<sub>p</sub>lorations of scalin<sub>g</sub> laws across model size and se<sub>q</sub>uence len<sub>g</sub>th (Fi<sub>g</sub>ure 5), and a dificult downstream s<sub>y</sub>nthetic classification task re<sub>q</sub>uirin<sub>g</sub> lon<sub>g</sub> context (Fi<sub>g</sub>ure 6).

For <sub>p</sub>retrainin<sub>g</sub>, we lar<sub>g</sub>el<sub>y</sub> follow a standard causal lan<sub>g</sub>ua<sub>g</sub>e modelin<sub>g</sub> (next token <sub>p</sub>rediction) setu<sub>p</sub> for the trainin<sub>g</sub> and model details (see also A<sub>pp</sub>endix E.2). For the dataset, we lar<sub>g</sub>el<sub>y</sub> follow the setu<sub>p</sub> of H<sub>y</sub>enaDNA (N<sub>g</sub>u<sub>y</sub>en, Poli, et al. 2023), which uses the HG38 dataset for <sub>p</sub>retrainin<sub>g</sub> consistin<sub>g</sub> of a sin<sub>g</sub>le human <sub>g</sub>enome with about 4.5 billion tokens (DNA base <sub>p</sub>airs) in the trainin<sub>g</sub> s<sub>p</sub>lit

## 4.3.1 Scaling: Model Size

## 4.3.2 Scaling: Context Length

In the next DNA ex<sub>p</sub>eriment<sub>,</sub> we investi<sub>g</sub>ate the scalin<sub>g p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>erties of models with res<sub>p</sub>ect to se<sub>q</sub>uence len<sub>g</sub>th. We onl<sub>y</sub> com<sub>p</sub>are the H<sub>y</sub>enaDNA and Mamba models<sub>,</sub> as <sub>q</sub>uadratic attention becomes <sub>p</sub>rohibitivel<sub>y</sub> ex<sub>p</sub>ensive at lon<sub>g</sub>er se<sub>q</sub>uence l<sub>eng</sub>th<sub>s.</sub> W<sub>e pre</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s on sequence</sub> l<sub>eng</sub>th<sub>s</sub> $2 ^ { 1 0 } \ = \ 1 0 2 4 , \ 2 ^ { 1 2 } \ = \ 4 0 9 6 , \ \dot { 2 } ^ { 1 4 } \ = \ 1 6 3 \dot { 8 } 4 , \ 2 ^ { \bar { 1 } 6 } \ = \ 6 5 5 3 6 , \ 2 ^ { \bar { 1 } 8 } \ = \ \hat { 2 } 6 2 1 4 4 ,$ $2 ^ { 2 0 } = 1 0 4 8 5 7 6$ . We fix a model size of 6 la<sub>y</sub>ers b<sub>y</sub> width 128 (about 1.3M-1.4M <sub>p</sub>arameters). Models were trained for 20� <sub>g</sub>radient ste<sub>p</sub>s for a total of ≈ 330� tokens. The lon<sub>g</sub>er se<sub>q</sub>uence len<sub>g</sub>ths used se<sub>q</sub>uence len<sub>g</sub>th warmu<sub>p</sub> similar to (N<sub>g</sub>u<sub>y</sub>en, Poli, et al. 2023).

Results. Figure 5 (Right) shows that Mamba is able to make use of longer context even up to extremely long sequences of length 1M, and its retrainin er lexit im roves as the context increases. On the other hand, the H<sub>y</sub>enaDNA model <sub>g</sub>ets worse with se<sub>qu</sub>ence len<sub>g</sub>th. This is int<sub>u</sub>itive from the disc<sub>u</sub>ssion in Section 3.5 on <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>erties of the selection mechanism. In <sub>p</sub>articular<sub>,</sub> LTI models cannot selectivel<sub>y</sub> i<sub>g</sub>nore information<sub>;</sub> from a convolutional <sub>p</sub>ers<sub>p</sub>ective<sub>,</sub> a ver<sub>y</sub> lon<sub>g</sub> convolution kernel is a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>atin<sub>g</sub> all information across a lon<sub>g</sub> se<sub>q</sub>uence which ma<sub>y</sub> be ver<sub>y</sub> nois<sub>y</sub>. Note tha while H<sub>y</sub>enaDNA claims to im<sub>p</sub>rove with lon<sub>g</sub>er context<sub>,</sub> their results do not control for com<sub>p</sub>utation time.

For the audio waveform modalit<sub>y</sub>, we com<sub>p</sub>are <sub>p</sub>rimaril<sub>y</sub> to the SaShiMi architecture and trainin<sub>g p</sub>rotocols (Goel et al. 2022). This model com<sub>p</sub>rises:

1<sub>.</sub> <sub>a</sub> U<sub>-</sub>N<sub>e</sub>t b<sub>ac</sub>kb<sub>one</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith t<sub>wo</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ages</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>poo</sub>li<sub>ng</sub> b<sub>y</sub> <sub>a</sub> f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>or</sub> <sub>�</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t d<sub>ou</sub>bl<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l di<sub>mens</sub>i<sub>on</sub> � <sub>per</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>age,</sub>

## 4.4.1 Long-Context Autoregressive Pretraining

We evaluate <sub>p</sub>retrainin<sub>g q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> (autore<sub>g</sub>ressive next-sam<sub>p</sub>le <sub>p</sub>rediction) on YouTubeMix (Dee<sub>p</sub>Sound 2017), a standard <sub>p</sub>iano music dataset used b<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>rior work consistin<sub>g</sub> of 4 hours of solo <sub>p</sub>iano music<sub>,</sub> sam<sub>p</sub>led at a rate of 16000 Hz. Pretrainin<sub>g</sub> details lar<sub>g</sub>el<sub>y</sub> follow the standard lan<sub>g</sub>ua<sub>g</sub>e modelin<sub>g</sub> setu<sub>p</sub> (Section 4.2). Fi<sub>g</sub>ure 7 evaluates the efect of increasin<sub>g</sub> trainin<sub>g</sub> <sub>sequence</sub> l<sub>eng</sub>th<sub>s</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> $2 ^ { 1 3 } = 8 1 9 2$ to 2<sup>20</sup> ≈ 10<sup>6</sup>, while kee<sub>p</sub>in<sub>g</sub> com<sub>p</sub>utation fixed. (There are some sli<sub>g</sub>ht ed<sub>g</sub>e cases to the <sub>way</sub> th<sub>e</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a</sub> i<sub>s cura</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d<sub>, w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>may</sub> l<sub>ea</sub>d t<sub>o</sub> ki<sub>n</sub>k<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e sca</sub>li<sub>ng curves.</sub> F<sub>or examp</sub>l<sub>e, on</sub>l<sub>y m</sub>i<sub>nu</sub>t<sub>e-</sub>l<sub>ong c</sub>li<sub>ps were ava</sub>il<sub>a</sub>bl <sub>so</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>max</sub>i<sub>mum</sub> <sub>sequence</sub> l<sub>eng</sub>th i<sub>s</sub> <sub>ac</sub>t<sub>ua</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> $6 0 s \cdot 1 6 0 0 0 H z = 9 6 0 0 0 0 . _ { . }$ )

Both Mamba and the SaShiMi (S4+MLP) baseline improve consistently with longer context lengths; Mamba is better throughout, and the gap widens at longer lengths. The main metric is bits er b te (BPB), which is a constant factor lo (2) of the standard ne ative lo -likelihood (NLL) loss for retrainin other modalities.

We note one im<sub>p</sub>ortant detail: this is the onl<sub>y</sub> ex<sub>p</sub>eriment in this <sub>p</sub>a<sub>p</sub>er in which we switched from the real <sub>p</sub>arameterization to com<sub>p</sub>lex (Section 3.6). We show additional ablations in A<sub>pp</sub>endix E.4.

## 4.4.2 Autoregressive Speech Generation

## 4.5 Speed and Memory Benchmarks

We benchmark the s<sub>p</sub>eed of the SSM scan o<sub>p</sub>eration (state ex<sub>p</sub>ansion � = 16), as well as the end-to-end inference throu<sub>g</sub>h<sub>p</sub>ut of Mamba<sub>,</sub> in Fi<sub>g</sub>ure 8. Our eficient SSM scan is faster than the best attention im<sub>p</sub>lementation that we know of (FlashAttention-2 (Dao 2024)) be<sub>y</sub>ond se<sub>q</sub>uence len<sub>g</sub>th 2K, and u<sub>p</sub> to 20-40× faster than a standard scan im<sub>p</sub>lementation in P<sub>y</sub>Torch. Mamba achieves 4-5× hi<sub>g</sub>her inference throu<sub>g</sub>h<sub>p</sub>ut than a Transformer of similar size, since without the KV cache it can use much hi her batch sizes. For exam le a Mamba-6.9B (untrained) would have hi her inference throu<sub>g</sub>h<sub>p</sub>ut than a 5× smaller Transformer-1.3B. Details in A<sub>pp</sub>endix E.5, which additionall<sub>y</sub> includes a benchmark of <sup>memor</sup>y <sup>consum</sup>p<sup>tion.</sup>

No Free Lunch: Continuous-Discrete Spectrum. Structured SSMs were originally defined as discretizations of continuous s<sub>y</sub>stems (1), and have had a stron<sub>g</sub> inductive bias toward continuous-time data modalities such as <sub>p</sub>erce<sub>p</sub>tual si<sub>g</sub>nals (e.<sub>g</sub>. audio, video). As discussed in Sections 3.1 and 3.5, the selection mechanism overcomes their weaknesses on discrete modalities s<sub>u</sub>ch as text and DNA<sub>;</sub> b<sub>u</sub>t this con<sub>v</sub>ersel<sub>y</sub> can im<sub>p</sub>ede their <sub>p</sub>erformance on data that LTI SSMs excel <sub>on.</sub> O<sub>ur a</sub>bl<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ons on au</sub>di<sub>o wave</sub>f<sub>orms exam</sub>i<sub>ne</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> t<sub>ra</sub>d<sub>eo</sub>f i<sub>n more</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>a</sub>il<sub>.</sub>

Downstream Afordances. Transformer-based foundation models (particularly LLMs) have a rich ecosystem of properties and modes of interaction with <sub>p</sub>retrained models<sub>,</sub> such as fine-tunin<sub>g,</sub> ada<sub>p</sub>tation<sub>, p</sub>rom<sub>p</sub>tin<sub>g,</sub> in-context learnin<sub>g,</sub> in<sub>s</sub>tr<sub>uc</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n t<sub>u</sub>nin<sub>g,</sub> RLHF<sub>, qua</sub>ntiz<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n<sub>, a</sub>nd <sub>so o</sub>n<sub>.</sub> W<sub>e a</sub>r<sub>e pa</sub>rti<sub>cu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>rl<sub>y</sub> int<sub>e</sub>r<sub>es</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d in <sub>w</sub>h<sub>e</sub>th<sub>e</sub>r Tr<sub>a</sub>n<sub>s</sub>f<sub>o</sub>rm<sub>e</sub>r <sub>a</sub>lt<sub>e</sub>rn<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ves suc</sub>h as SSMs have similar <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>erties and afordances.

Scaling. Our empirical evaluation is limited to small model sizes, below the threshold of most strong open source LLMs (e.<sub>g</sub>. Llama (Touvron et al. 2023)) as well as other recurrent models such as RWKV (B. Pen<sub>g</sub> et al. 2023) and RetNet (Y. Sun et al. 2023), which have been evaluated at the 7B <sub>p</sub>arameter scale and be<sub>y</sub>ond. It remains to assess whether Mamba stil com<sub>p</sub>ares favorabl<sub>y</sub> at these lar<sub>g</sub>er sizes. We also note that scalin<sub>g</sub> SSMs ma<sub>y</sub> involve further en<sub>g</sub>ineerin<sub>g</sub> challen<sub>g</sub>es and a<sup>d</sup>justments to t<sup>h</sup>e mo<sup>d</sup>e<sup>l</sup> t<sup>h</sup>at are not <sup>d</sup>iscusse<sup>d</sup> in t<sup>h</sup>is paper.

## A Discussion: Selection Mechanism

Our selection mechanism is ins<sub>p</sub>ired b<sub>y</sub> and related to conce<sub>p</sub>ts such as <sub>g</sub>atin<sub>g,</sub> h<sub>yp</sub>ernetworks<sub>,</sub> and data-de<sub>p</sub>endence. It can also be viewed as related to “fast wei<sub>g</sub>hts” (J. Ba et al. 2016; Schmidhuber 1992), which connects classical RNNs with the mechanism of linear attention (Schla<sub>g</sub>, Irie, and Schmidhuber 2021). However, we believe that it is a distinct conce<sub>p</sub>t that is worth clarif<sub>y</sub>in<sub>g</sub>.

Gating. Gating originally referred to the gating mechanisms of RNNs such as the LSTM (Hochreiter and Schmidhuber 1997) and GRU (J. Chun<sub>g</sub> et al. 2014), or the <sub>g</sub>ated e<sub>q</sub>uation (5) in Theorem 1. This was inter<sub>p</sub>reted as a <sub>p</sub>articular mechanism for controllin<sub>g</sub> whether to let an in<sub>p</sub>ut into the hidden state of an RNN. In <sub>p</sub>articular<sub>,</sub> this afects the <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>a<sub>g</sub>ation of si<sub>g</sub>nal throu<sub>g</sub>h time and causes in<sub>p</sub>uts to interact alon<sub>g</sub> the se<sub>q</sub>uence len<sub>g</sub>th dimension.

However<sub>,</sub> the conce<sub>p</sub>t of <sub>g</sub>atin<sub>g</sub> has since been relaxed in <sub>p</sub>o<sub>p</sub>ular usa<sub>g</sub>e to sim<sub>p</sub>l<sub>y</sub> mean an<sub>y</sub> multi<sub>p</sub>licative interaction (often with an activation function). For example, elementwise multiplicative components of neural network architectures (that do not interact alon<sub>g</sub> se<sub>q</sub>uence len<sub>g</sub>th) are now commonl<sub>y</sub> referred to as <sub>g</sub>ated architectures (Hua et al. 2022; Mehta et al. 2023), despite a very diferent meaning than the original RNN sense. Thus we believe the original concept of RNN gating versus the popular usage of multiplicative gating actually have a very diferent semantic meaning.

Hypernetworks. Hypernetworks refer to neural networks whose parameters are themselves generated by smaller neural networks. The ori<sub>g</sub>inal idea (Ha, Dai, and Quoc V. Le 2017) used it in a narrow sense to define a lar<sub>g</sub>e RNN whose recurrent <sub>p</sub>arameters are <sub>g</sub>enerated b<sub>y</sub> a smaller RNN, and other variants have been around for a lon<sub>g</sub> time (Schmidhuber 1992).

Data-dependence. Similar to hypernetworks, data-dependence can refer to any notion where some parameters of the model de<sub>p</sub>end on the data (Poli et al. 2023).

Example: GLU Activation. To illustrate the issues with these concepts, consider a simple diagonal linear layer � = ��, where � is a diagonal weight parameter. Now suppose that � is itself generated from a linear transformation of �, with an o<sub>p</sub>tional nonlinearit<sub>y</sub>: � = �(��). Since it is dia<sub>g</sub>onal, the multi<sub>p</sub>lication becomes an elementwise <sub>p</sub>roduct: <sub>�</sub> = �(��) ◦ �.

This is a rather trivial transformation, <sub>y</sub>et it technicall<sub>y</sub> satisfies the common meanin<sub>g</sub>s of <sub>g</sub>atin<sub>g</sub> (since it has a multi<sub>p</sub>licative “branch”), h<sub>yp</sub>ernetworks (since the <sub>p</sub>arameter � is <sub>g</sub>enerated b<sub>y</sub> another la<sub>y</sub>er), and data-de<sub>p</sub>endent (since � de<sub>p</sub>ends on the data �). However, this in fact simply defines a GLU function, which is so simple that it is often considered just an activation function (Dau<sub>p</sub>hin et al. 2017; Shazeer 2020) instead of a meanin<sub>g</sub>ful la<sub>y</sub>er

Selection. Thus, while selection mechanisms could be considered a special case of ideas such as architectural gating, h<sub>yperne</sub>t<sub>wor</sub>k<sub>s, or</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a-</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>ence, so can an enormous range o</sub>f <sub>o</sub>th<sub>er cons</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>ti<sub>ons—essen</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>y any</sub>thi<sub>ng w</sub>ith <sub>a</sub> multi<sub>p</sub>lication, includin<sub>g</sub> standard attention mechanisms (Bahdanau, Cho, and Ben<sub>g</sub>io 2015; Vaswani et al. 2017) as <sub>we</sub>ll<sub>—an</sub>d <sub>we</sub> fi<sub>n</sub>d it <sub>un</sub>i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>orma</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> t<sub>o</sub> thi<sub>n</sub>k <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>em as suc</sub>h<sub>.</sub>

Instead, we view it as most closel related to the atin mechanism of traditional RNNs, which is a s ecial case (Theorem 1) and also has a dee<sub>p</sub>er histor<sub>y</sub> of connections to SSMs throu<sub>g</sub>h variable (in<sub>p</sub>ut-de<sub>p</sub>endent) discretization of Δ (Funahashi and Nakamura 1993; Gu, Dao, et al. 2020; Tallec and Ollivier 2018). We also eschew the term “gating” in favor of selection to clarify the overloaded use of former. More narrowly, we use selection to refer to the mechanistic action of a model to select or i<sub>g</sub>nore in<sub>p</sub>uts and facilitate data interaction alon<sub>g</sub> the se<sub>q</sub>uence len<sub>g</sub>th (Section 3.1). Be<sub>y</sub>ond selective SSMs and <sub>g</sub>ated RNNs, other exam<sub>p</sub>les ma<sub>y</sub> include in<sub>p</sub>ut-de<sub>p</sub>endent convolutions (Kosma, Nikolentzos, and Vazir<sub>g</sub>iannis 2023; Lioutas and Guo 2020; Lutati, Zimerman, and Wolf 2023; Yan<sub>g</sub> et al. 2019) and even attention.

## B.3 Relationship to RNNs

RNNs and SSMs are broadly related, as they both involve the concepts of recurrence on a latent state.

Several older RNNs such as the stron<sub>g</sub>l<sub>y</sub> t<sub>yp</sub>ed RNN (Balduzzi and Ghifar<sub>y</sub> 2016), <sub>q</sub>uasi-RNN (QRNN) (Bradbur<sub>y</sub> et al. 2016), and sim<sub>p</sub>le recurrent unit (SRU) (Lei 2021; Lei et al. 2017) involve forms of <sub>g</sub>ated RNNs without time-wise nonlinearities. Because of the connections of <sub>g</sub>atin<sub>g</sub> mechanisms and selection mechanisms<sub>,</sub> these can be viewed as cases of selective SSMs<sub>,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>are</sub> th<sub>us more ower</sub>f<sub>u</sub>l i<sub>n a sense</sub> th<sub>an</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>am</sub>il <sub>o</sub>f LTI <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ure</sub>d SSM<sub>s a</sub>b<sub>ove.</sub> Th<sub>e ma</sub>i<sub>n</sub> dif<sub>erences are:</sub>

• The<sub>y</sub> do not use state ex<sub>p</sub>ansion (� = 1) or selective �, � <sub>p</sub>arameters, both of which are im<sub>p</sub>ortant for <sub>p</sub>erformance (Section 4.6).

• The<sub>y</sub> use a heuristic <sub>g</sub>atin<sub>g</sub> mechanism<sub>,</sub> which we <sub>g</sub>eneralize as a conse<sub>q</sub>uence of the selection mechanism + discretization (Theorem 1). The connections to <sub>p</sub>rinci<sub>p</sub>led SSM theor<sub>y p</sub>rovides better <sub>p</sub>arameterizations and initializations (Section 3.6).

Additionall<sub>y</sub>, older RNNs famousl<sub>y</sub> sufered from eficienc<sub>y</sub> issues and the vanishin<sub>g g</sub>radients <sub>p</sub>roblem (Hochreiter 1991; Hochreiter, Ben<sub>g</sub>io, et al. 2001; Pascanu, Mikolov, and Ben<sub>g</sub>io 2013), both caused b<sub>y</sub> their se<sub>q</sub>uential nature. The former could be solved for some of the above RNNs b<sub>y</sub> levera<sub>g</sub>in<sub>g</sub> the <sub>p</sub>arallel scan (Martin and Cund<sub>y</sub> 2018), but the latter <sub>was</sub> difi<sub>cu</sub>lt <sub>w</sub>ith<sub>ou</sub>t th<sub>eory</sub> l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>er</sub> d<sub>eve</sub>l<sub>ope</sub>d f<sub>or</sub> SSM<sub>s.</sub> F<sub>or examp</sub>l<sub>e, mo</sub>d<sub>ern s</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ure</sub>d SSM<sub>s</sub> dif<sub>er</sub> i<sub>n more care</sub>f<sub>u</sub>l <sub>p</sub>arameterization of the recurrent d<sub>y</sub>namics ins<sub>p</sub>ired b<sub>y</sub> classical SSM theor<sub>y</sub> (e.<sub>g</sub>. throu<sub>g</sub>h discretization (Gu, Johnson, Goel, et al. 2021; Gu, Johnson, Timalsina, et al. 2023)), or direct anal<sub>y</sub>sis (Gu<sub>p</sub>ta, Mehta, and Berant 2022; Kaul 2020; Orvieto et al. 2023)).

We also note that there is a long line of work on orthogonal RNNs (Arjovsky, Shah, and Bengio 2016; Henaf, Szlam, and LeCun 2016; Lezcano-Casado and Martínez-Rubio 2019; Mhammedi et al. 2017; Vorontsov et al. 2017) which are motivated by constraining the � transition matrix to be orthogonal or unitary, in order to control its eigenvalues and <sub>reven</sub>t th<sub>e van</sub>i<sub>s</sub>hi<sub>n ra</sub>di<sub>en</sub>t <sub>ro</sub>bl<sub>em.</sub> H<sub>owever,</sub> th<sub>ese</sub> h<sub>a</sub>d <sub>o</sub>th<sub>er</sub> li<sub>m</sub>it<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ons; we</sub> b<sub>e</sub>li<sub>eve</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t th<sub>ese s</sub>t<sub>em</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>ac</sub>t that ortho<sub>g</sub>onal/unitar<sub>y</sub> RNNs are also LTI. For exam<sub>p</sub>le<sub>,</sub> the<sub>y</sub> are almost alwa<sub>y</sub>s evaluated on the Co<sub>py</sub>in<sub>g</sub> task which the<sub>y</sub> can solve erfectl , but observed to stru le on the Selective Co in task (Jin et al. 2019).

## B.4 Linear Attention

The Linear Attention (LA) (Katharo<sub>p</sub>oulos et al. 2020) framework is an im<sub>p</sub>ortant result <sub>p</sub>o<sub>p</sub>ularizin<sub>g</sub> kernel attention and <sub>s</sub>h<sub>ow</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> h<sub>ow</sub> it <sub>re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>recurren</sub>t <sub>au</sub>t<sub>oregress</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s.</sub> M<sub>any</sub> <sub>var</sub>i<sub>an</sub>t<sub>s</sub> h<sub>ave</sub> <sub>propose</sub>d <sub>a</sub>lt<sub>erna</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> k<sub>erne</sub>l<sub>s</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>o</sub>th<sub>er</sub> modifications. Random Feature Attention (RFA) (H. Pen<sub>g</sub> et al. 2021) chooses the kernel feature ma<sub>p</sub> to a<sub>pp</sub>roximate softmax attention (i.e. the ex<sub>p</sub> feature ma<sub>p</sub>) usin<sub>g</sub> the random Fourier feature a<sub>pp</sub>roximation of Gaussian kernels (Rahimi and Recht 2007). Performer (Choromanski et al. 2021) finds an a<sub>pp</sub>roximation to the ex<sub>p</sub>onential kernel involvin<sub>g</sub> onl<sub>y p</sub>ositive features, which also allows the softmax normalization term. TransNormer (Qin, Han, W. Sun, D. Li, et al. 2022) showed that the LA denominator term can be unstable and proposed replacing it with a LayerNorm. cosFormer (Qin, W. Sun, et al. 2022) au<sub>g</sub>ments RFA with a cosine rewei<sub>g</sub>htin<sub>g</sub> mechanism that incor<sub>p</sub>orates <sub>p</sub>ositional information to em<sub>p</sub>hasize localit<sub>y</sub>. Linear Randomized Attention (Zhen<sub>g</sub>, C. Wan<sub>g</sub>, and L. Kon<sub>g</sub> 2022) <sub>g</sub>eneralize RFA from the <sub>p</sub>ers<sub>p</sub>ective of im<sub>p</sub>ortance sampling, and generalize it to provide better estimates of the full softmax kernel (rather than just the exp-transformed numerator).

Aside from kernel attention, man<sub>y</sub> other variants of eficient attention exist; the surve<sub>y</sub> Ta<sub>y</sub>, Deh<sub>g</sub>hani, Bahri, et al. (2022) ofers an extensive cate<sub>g</sub>orization of man<sub>y</sub> of these.

## B.5 Long Context Models

Lon context <sup>h</sup>as <sup>b</sup>ecome a o u<sup>l</sup>ar su<sup>b</sup>ject, an<sup>d</sup> severa<sup>l</sup> recent mo<sup>d</sup>e<sup>l</sup>s <sup>h</sup>ave c<sup>l</sup>aime<sup>d</sup> to sca<sup>l</sup>e to <sup>l</sup>on er an<sup>d l</sup>on er se uences. H<sub>owever,</sub> th<sub>ese are o</sub>ft<sub>en</sub> f<sub>rom a compu</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ona</sub>l <sub>s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>d<sub>po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t <sub>an</sub>d h<sub>ave no</sub>t b<sub>een ex</sub>t<sub>ens</sub>i<sub>ve</sub>l<sub>y va</sub>lid<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d<sub>.</sub> Th<sub>ese</sub> i<sub>nc</sub>l<sub>u</sub>d<sub>e:</sub>

• Recurrent Memor<sub>y</sub> Transformer (Bulatov, Kuratov, and Burtsev 2023), a li<sub>g</sub>htwei<sub>g</sub>ht wra<sub>pp</sub>er around a Transformer backbone. ## C Mechanics of Selective SSMs

ProofofTheorem 1. Consider a selective SSM (Algorithm 2) with $N = 1 , A = - 1 , B = 1 , s _ { \Delta } = \mathsf { L i n e a r } ( x ) , \tau _ { \Delta } = \mathsf { s o f t p l u s } .$ Th<sub>e</sub> corres<sub>p</sub>ondin<sub>g</sub> continuous-time SSM (1) is

$$
h (t) = - h (t) + x (t)
$$

which is also called a leaky integrator.

Th<sub>e</sub> di<sub>sc</sub>r<sub>e</sub>tiz<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ep s</sub>iz<sub>e</sub> i<sub>s</sub>

$$
\begin{array}{r l} \Delta_ {t} & = \tau_ {\Delta} (\text { Parameter } + s _ {\Delta} (x _ {t})) \\ & = \text { softplus } (\text { Parameter } + \text { Linear } (x _ {t})) \\ & = \text { softplus } (\text { Linear } (x _ {t})) \end{array}
$$

w<sup>h</sup>ere we o<sup>b</sup>serve t<sup>h</sup>at t<sup>h</sup>e parameter can <sup>b</sup>e viewe<sup>d</sup> as a <sup>l</sup>earna<sup>bl</sup>e <sup>b</sup>ias an<sup>d</sup> <sup>f</sup>o<sup>ld</sup>e<sup>d</sup> into t<sup>h</sup>e <sup>l</sup>inear projection.

Now a<sub>pp</sub>l<sub>y</sub>in<sub>g</sub> the zero-order hold (ZOH) discretization formulas:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{A}} _ {t} = \exp (\Delta A) = \frac {1}{1 + \exp (\operatorname{Linear} (x _ {t}))} = \sigma (- \operatorname{Linear} (x _ {t})) \\ \qquad = 1 - \sigma (\operatorname{Linear} (x _ {t})) \\ \overline {{B}} _ {t} = (\Delta A) ^ {- 1} (\exp (\Delta A) - I) \cdot \Delta B = - (\exp (\Delta A) - I) = 1 - \overline {{A}} \\ \qquad = \sigma (\operatorname{Linear} (x _ {t})). \end{array}
$$

Thus the final discrete recurrence (2a) is

$$
g _ {t} = \sigma (\operatorname{Linear} (x _ {t}))
$$

$$
h _ {t} = (1 - g _ {t}) h _ {t - 1} + g _ {t} x _ {t}
$$

<sub>as</sub> d<sub>es</sub>i<sub>re</sub>d<sub>.</sub>

## D Hardware-aware Algorithm For Selective SSMs

Without in<sub>p</sub>ut-de<sub>p</sub>endent selectivit<sub>y</sub>, SSMs can be eficientl<sub>y</sub> im<sub>p</sub>lemented as a convolution (Dao, Fu, Saab, et al. 2023; Gu, Goel, and Ré 2022), which levera<sub>g</sub>es the fast Fourier transform (FFT) as <sub>p</sub>rimitive. With selectivit<sub>y</sub>, SSMs are no-lon<sub>g</sub>er <sub>e u</sub>i<sub>va</sub>l<sub>en</sub>t t<sub>o convo</sub>l<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> b<sub>u</sub>t <sub>we</sub> l<sub>evera e</sub> th<sub>e ara</sub>ll<sub>e</sub>l <sub>assoc</sub>i<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ve scan.</sub> Whil<sub>e</sub> SSM <sub>scans are</sub> th<sub>eore</sub>ti<sub>ca</sub>ll <sub>e</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>en</sub>t (�(����) FLOPs, scalin<sub>g</sub> linear in �), trainin<sub>g</sub> foundation models with selective SSMs re<sub>q</sub>uires them to be eficient on modern hardware (GPUs) as well. We describe how we use kernel fusion and recomputation to make SSM scan fast and memor<sub>y</sub>-eficient. We evaluate the s<sub>p</sub>eed of our scan im<sub>p</sub>lementation com<sub>p</sub>ared to convolution and attention in Section 4.5<sub>,</sub> showin<sub>g</sub> that it is u<sub>p</sub> to 7× times faster than attention at se<sub>q</sub>uence len<sub>g</sub>th 32K, and is as memor<sub>y</sub>-eficient as the best attention im<sub>p</sub>lementation (FlashAttention).

Speed. On modern hardware accelerators (GPUs) most operations (except matrix multiply) are bounded by memorybandwidth (Dao, Fu, Ermon, et al. 2022; Ivanov et al. 2021; Williams, Waterman, and Patterson 2009). This the case with our scan o<sub>p</sub>eration<sub>,</sub> and we use kernel fusion to reduce the amount of memor<sub>y</sub> IOs<sub>,</sub> leadin<sub>g</sub> to si<sub>g</sub>nificant s<sub>p</sub>eedu<sub>p</sub> com<sub>p</sub>ared to <sub>a s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>d<sub>ar</sub>d i<sub>mp</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub>

The standard wa<sub>y</sub> to im<sub>p</sub>lement the scan al<sub>g</sub>orithm in Section 3.2 is to <sub>p</sub>re<sub>p</sub>are the scan in<sub>p</sub>ut $\overline { { A } } , \overline { { B } }$ <sub>o</sub>f <sub>s</sub>i<sub>ze</sub> $( B , L , D , N )$ in GPU HBM (hi<sub>g</sub>h-bandwidth memor<sub>y</sub>, commonl<sub>y</sub> referred to as GPU memor<sub>y</sub>), call a <sub>p</sub>arallel associative scan im<sub>p</sub>lementation to write the scan out<sub>p</sub>ut of size $( B , L , D , N )$ to GPU HBM, then multiply that scan output with � to produce an output of size (�, �, �). However, this re<sub>q</sub>uires the number of memor<sub>y</sub> reads/writes on the order of �(����). We can instead fuse the discretization step, the scan, and the multiplication with � into one kernel:

1. We read in �(��� + ��) bytes of memory (Δ, �, �, �) from slow HBM to fast SRAM.

2. We discretize to produce �, � of size (�, �, �, �) in SRAM.

3. We <sub>p</sub>erform a <sub>p</sub>arallel associative scan, <sub>y</sub>ieldin<sub>g</sub> intermediate states of size (�, �, �, �) in SRAM.

4. We multi<sub>p</sub>l<sub>y</sub> and sum with �, <sub>p</sub>roducin<sub>g</sub> out<sub>p</sub>uts of size (�, �, �) and write it to HBM.

This wa<sub>y</sub>, we reduce IOs b<sub>y</sub> a factor of �(�) (the state dimension), which in <sub>p</sub>ractice s<sub>p</sub>eeds u<sub>p</sub> the o<sub>p</sub>eration b<sub>y</sub> 20-40 times (Section 4.5).

For se uence len th � too lon where we cannot fit the se uence in SRAM (which is much smaller than HBM), we s lit the <sub>sequences</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o c</sub>h<sub>un</sub>k<sub>s an</sub>d <sub>per</sub>f<sub>orm</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>use</sub>d <sub>scan on eac</sub>h <sub>c</sub>h<sub>un</sub>k<sub>.</sub> A<sub>s</sub> l<sub>ong as we</sub> h<sub>ave</sub> th<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erme</sub>di<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e scan s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es, we can</sub> <sub>con</sub>ti<sub>nue</sub> th<sub>e scan w</sub>ith th<sub>e nex</sub>t <sub>c</sub>h<sub>un</sub>k<sub>.</sub>

Memory. We describe how we use the classical technique of recomputation to reduce the total amount of memory re<sub>qu</sub>ired to train selecti<sub>v</sub>e SSM la<sub>y</sub>ers.

From the wa<sub>y</sub> we fuse the forward <sub>p</sub>ass, we do not save the intermediate states of size (�, �, �, �) to avoid memor<sub>y</sub> blowu<sub>p</sub>. However<sub>,</sub> these intermediate states are necessar<sub>y</sub> for the backward <sub>p</sub>ass to com<sub>p</sub>ute <sub>g</sub>radients. We instead recom<sub>p</sub>ute those intermediate states in the backward pass. Since the inputs Δ, �, �, � and output gradient read from HBM to SRAM are of size �(��� + ��), and the in<sub>p</sub>ut <sub>g</sub>radients are also of size �(��� + ��), recom<sub>p</sub>utation avoids the cost of readin<sub>g</sub> �(����) elements from HBM. This means that recom<sub>p</sub>utation of the SSM states in the backward <sub>p</sub>ass s<sub>p</sub>eeds u<sub>p</sub> the <sub>com u</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on com are</sub>d t<sub>o s</sub>t<sub>or</sub>i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>em an</sub>d <sub>rea</sub>di<sub>n</sub> th<sub>em</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> HBM

Beyon<sup>d</sup> optimizing <sup>f</sup>or t<sup>h</sup>e memory requirement o<sup>f</sup> just t<sup>h</sup>e scan operation, we a<sup>l</sup>so use recomputation to optimize t<sup>h</sup>e memory requirement of the entire selective SSM block (input projection, convolution, activation, scan, output projection). In <sub>p</sub>articular, we do not save intermediate activations that take a lot of memor<sub>y</sub> but are fast to recom<sub>p</sub>ute (e.<sub>g</sub>. out<sub>p</sub>ut of activation function or short convolution). As a result, the selective SSM la<sub>y</sub>er has the same memor<sub>y</sub> re<sub>q</sub>uirement as an o<sub>p</sub>timized Transformer im<sub>p</sub>lementation with FlashAttention. In <sub>p</sub>articular, each attention la<sub>y</sub>er (FlashAttention) stores <sub>aroun</sub>d 12 b<sub>y</sub>t<sub>es o</sub>f <sub>ac</sub>ti<sub>va</sub>ti<sub>ons per</sub> t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>en, an eac</sub>h MLP l<sub>ayer s</sub>t<sub>ores aroun</sub>d 20 b<sub>y</sub>t<sub>es o</sub>f <sub>ac</sub>ti<sub>va</sub>ti<sub>ons per</sub> t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>en,</sub> f<sub>or a</sub> t<sub>o</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l <sub>o</sub>f 32 b<sub>y</sub>tes ((assumin<sub>g</sub> mixed-<sub>p</sub>recision trainin<sub>g</sub> in FP16 or BF16)). Each selective SSM stores around 16 b<sub>y</sub>tes of activations <sub>p</sub>er token. Hence two la<sub>y</sub>ers of selective SSMs have around the same activation memor<sub>y</sub> as an attention la<sub>y</sub>er and an MLP <sup>l</sup>a<sub>y</sub>er.

## E Experimental Details and Additional Results

## E.1 Synthetic Tasks

Selective Copying. Our setting is on sequences of length 4096, with a vocab size of 16 possible tokens (including the white “noise” token from Fi<sub>g</sub>ure 2) and re<sub>q</sub>uirin<sub>g</sub> models to memorize 16 “data” tokens. We use 2 la<sub>y</sub>er models with a model di<sub>mens</sub>i<sub>on o</sub>f $D = 6 4$

M<sub>o</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s</sub> <sub>are</sub> t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>ne</sub>d f<sub>or</sub> 400K <sub>s</sub>t<sub>eps</sub> <sub>a</sub>t <sub>a</sub> <sub>cons</sub>t<sub>an</sub>t l<sub>earn</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>o</sub>f 0<sub>.</sub>0001 <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>a</sub> b<sub>a</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h <sub>s</sub>i<sub>ze</sub> <sub>o</sub>f 64<sub>.</sub>

Induction Heads. Training consists of randomly generating data every step, with a batch size of 8. We choose an “e<sub>p</sub>och” size of 8192 ste<sub>p</sub>s, and track the accurac<sub>y</sub> on fixed validation sets (also randoml<sub>y</sub> <sub>g</sub>enerated) of each tar<sub>g</sub>et se<sub>q</sub>uence len<sub>g</sub>th. For the MHA-Abs and Mamba models, results are re<sub>p</sub>orted after the 25th e<sub>p</sub>och (8192 × 25 = 204800 ste<sub>p</sub>s). For the MHA-RoPE and MHA-xPos models, results are re<sub>p</sub>orted after the 50th e<sub>p</sub>och (8192 × 50 = 409600 ste<sub>p</sub>s). For the LTI H3 and H<sub>y</sub>ena models, results are re<sub>p</sub>orted after the 10th e<sub>p</sub>och (81920 ste<sub>p</sub>s) because the<sub>y</sub> had conver<sub>g</sub>ed b<sub>y</sub> then and failed t<sub>o</sub> i<sub>mprove</sub> f<sub>ur</sub>th<sub>er.</sub>

We use the Adam o<sub>p</sub>timizer with no wei<sub>g</sub>ht deca<sub>y</sub>. All models are trained at constant learnin<sub>g</sub> rates 2� − 4 and 1� − 3, and the better results are re<sub>p</sub>orted for each model (2� − 4 for all models exce<sub>p</sub>t Mamba). The attention and H<sub>y</sub>ena models did not learn at LR 1� − 3. H3 learned at both LRs, but interestin<sub>g</sub>l<sub>y g</sub>eneralized better to shorter se<sub>q</sub>uences at the smaller LR of 2� − 4. Mamba learned at both LRs, but extra<sub>p</sub>olated better at the lar<sub>g</sub>er LR of 1� − 3.

## Architecture and Training Details. Our models are:

• Transformer: The standard Transformer based on GPT3 (Table 12).

• Transformer++: A Transformer with an im roved architecture, namel rotar ositional encodings (Su et al. 2021) and SwiGLU MLP (Shazeer 2020), and the im<sub>p</sub>roved trainin<sub>g</sub> reci<sub>p</sub>e above.

• Hyena: Interleaving a Hyena block (the H3 block with S4 replaced by a global convolution parameterized by an MLP) with <sub>s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>d<sub>ar</sub>d MLP bl<sub>oc</sub>k<sub>s.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> MLP bl<sub>oc</sub>k<sub>s</sub> h<sub>ave expans</sub>i<sub>on</sub> f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>or</sub> 2 i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ea</sub>d <sub>o</sub>f 4 <sub>an</sub>d th<sub>e num</sub>b<sub>er o</sub>f l<sub>ayers</sub> i<sub>s correspon</sub>di<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>y</sub> increased b<sub>y</sub> 1.5× to <sub>p</sub>reserve <sub>p</sub>arameter count.

• H3++: The H3 architecture with a few modifications, including (i) using the same “thin” Hyena dimensions above (ii) the improved training recipe above (iii) a linear attention head dimension of 8.

• RWKV: The default RWKV model from B. Peng et al. (2023), including its modified MLP block. We also used as much of its s<sub>p</sub>ecified trainin<sub>g</sub> reci<sub>p</sub>e as <sub>p</sub>ossible, such as increasin<sub>g</sub> the learnin<sub>g</sub> rates b<sub>y</sub> 2× or 3× on certain <sub>p</sub>arameters.

• RetNet: The default RetNet model from Y. Sun et al. (2023). We also gave it the improved training recipe above.

• Mamba: The standard Mamba architecture, with the improved training recipe.

This <sub>p</sub>retrainin<sub>g p</sub>roced<sub>u</sub>re is the same as the scalin<sub>g</sub> law <sub>p</sub>rotocol<sub>,</sub> b<sub>u</sub>t extended to 300B tokens and with the GPT-NeoX tokenizer (Black et al. 2022) instead of GPT2 tokenizer. We re<sub>p</sub>ort the <sub>p</sub>er<sub>p</sub>lexit<sub>y</sub> on the Pile validation set<sub>,</sub> and for this metric onl<sub>y</sub> com<sub>p</sub>are to models trained on the same dataset and <sub>w</sub>ith the same tokenizer<sub>,</sub> in <sub>p</sub>artic<sub>u</sub>lar P<sub>y</sub>thia and RWKV.

For downstream evaluation, we use the LM evaluation harness from EleutherAI (L. Gao, Tow, et al. 2021), as done b<sub>y</sub> most <sub>wor</sub>k i<sub>n</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> <sub>area.</sub> W<sub>e</sub> <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ow</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>s</sub>/d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t<sub>s</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>measure</sub> <sub>common</sub> <sub>sense</sub> <sub>reason</sub>i<sub>ng:</sub>

## E.3.2 Scaling: Model Size Details

Models. The models we consider are:

• Transformer++: a Transformer with im roved architecture, notabl the usa e of RoPE ositional encodin s (Su et al. 2021). Informall<sub>y</sub>, we found these to be noticeabl<sub>y</sub> better than vanilla <sub>p</sub>ositional encodin<sub>g</sub>s from (Vaswani et al. 2017).

• H<sub>y</sub>enaDNA: the H<sub>y</sub>ena model from N<sub>g</sub>u<sub>y</sub>en, Poli, et al. (2023) and Poli et al. (2023), which is rou<sub>g</sub>hl<sub>y</sub> a Transformer with th<sub>e</sub> MHA bl<sub>oc</sub>k <sub>rep</sub>l<sub>ace</sub>d b<sub>y an</sub> H3 bl<sub>oc</sub>k <sub>us</sub>i<sub>ng a g</sub>l<sub>o</sub>b<sub>a</sub>l <sub>convo</sub>l<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on parame</sub>t<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ze</sub>d b<sub>y an</sub> MLP<sub>.</sub>

Training. For each model (Transformer++, HyenaDNA, Mamba), we swept the learning rate across $\{ 1 e - 3 , 2 e - 3 , 4 e -$ $3 , 8 e - 3 \}$ <sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e o</sub> ti<sub>ma</sub>l T<sub>rans</sub>f<sub>ormer an</sub>d H <sub>ena</sub>DNA l<sub>earn</sub>i<sub>n ra</sub>t<sub>es were</sub> 2<sub>e-</sub>3 <sub>across a</sub>ll <sub>s</sub>i<sub>zes.</sub> Th<sub>e o</sub> ti<sub>ma</sub>l M<sub>am</sub>b<sub>a</sub> l<sub>earn</sub>i<sub>n</sub> <sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>was</sub> 8<sub>e-</sub>3<sub>;</sub> <sub>no</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t M<sub>am</sub>b<sub>a</sub> <sub>per</sub>f<sub>orme</sub>d b<sub>e</sub>tt<sub>er</sub> th<sub>an</sub> b<sub>ase</sub>li<sub>nes</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d l<sub>earn</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>ra</sub>t<sub>es</sub> $( 2 \mathrm { e } \mathrm { - } 3 )$ <sub>,</sub> b<sub>u</sub>t <sub>was more s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> and im<sub>p</sub>roved even more at hi<sub>g</sub>her learnin<sub>g</sub> rates. (Furthermore, as this LR is on the u<sub>pp</sub>er ran<sub>g</sub>e of the swee<sub>p</sub>, it is <sub>p</sub>ossible that our results are still subo<sub>p</sub>timal.)

Note that, in contrast to standard LM scalin<sub>g</sub> laws (Table 12), our LR held constant across model sizes for sim<sub>p</sub>licit<sub>y</sub>. The o<sub>p</sub>timal LR should <sub>g</sub>o down for lar<sub>g</sub>er models, but we didn’t find a noticeable efect at the small model sizes (at most a few million <sub>p</sub>arameters) we considered.

Sequence Length Warmup. Following (Nguyen, Poli, et al. 2023), we use sequence length warmup (SLW) during <sub>p</sub>retrainin<sub>g</sub>. We choose a sim<sub>p</sub>le schedule of 2 e<sub>p</sub>ochs at each <sub>p</sub>ower-of-two se<sub>q</sub>uence len<sub>g</sub>th startin<sub>g</sub> from $2 ^ { 1 0 } = 1 0 2 4$ (Note that because of how data is curated, at the lon<sub>g</sub>est se<sub>q</sub>uence len<sub>g</sub>ths more ste<sub>p</sub>s and tokens are s<sub>p</sub>ent <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>ortionall<sub>y</sub>. In <sub>p</sub>articular<sub>,</sub> each sta<sub>g</sub>e u<sub>p</sub> to len<sub>g</sub>th $2 ^ { 1 7 }$ <sub>p</sub>rocesses the same number of tokens, but 4× as man<sub>y</sub> tokens are <sub>p</sub>rocessed at l<sub>eng</sub>th $2 ^ { 1 8 }$ , 8× as man<sub>y</sub> at len<sub>g</sub>th $2 ^ { \overset { \smile } { 1 9 } }$ , and 16× as man<sub>y</sub> at len<sub>g</sub>th $2 ^ { 2 0 } .$ .)

Remark E.1. We also note that the schedule was not tuned, and we never experimented with turning ofsequence length warmup for these pretraining experiments. We later found that SLW did not help noticeably for audio pretraining at similar lengths (Section 4.4), and it is possible that it is not necessary for DNA pretraining either.

## E.4 Audio Details

## E.4.1 YouTubeMix Audio Pretraining

Model. We use a model with 3 blocks per stage $( 3 \times 5 = 1 5$ total Mamba blocks), <sub>p</sub>oolin<sub>g</sub> factor $p = 1 6 ,$ <sub>,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>ou</sub>t<sub>er</sub> dim<sub>e</sub>n<sub>s</sub>i<sub>o</sub>n $D = 6 4$ <sub>,</sub> f<sub>or a</sub>b<sub>ou</sub>t 3<sub>.</sub>5M <sub>parame</sub>t<sub>ers.</sub>

Dataset. The data is mu-law encoded at 8 bits, so the model is modeling discrete tokens with a vocab size of 256.
